\textbf{Research gap.} Among the reviewed studies, none reports a directly comparable n1(+15) forecast evaluation on the released SCADA. Leak diagnosis, current reconstruction and forecasts with other data/horizons answer different questions. We therefore compare Gradient Boosting, persistence, Ridge and Random Forest on common chronological splits and test auxiliary-signal value. This provides reproducible classical baselines for future comparison with complex models, which are not reproduced here.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\newpage
\milestoneheader{2}{Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting}{4/5}
\sectiontitle{3. Your Anticipated Contributions}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.1 Anticipated contribution relative to prior work}\par\vspace{.7mm}
We propose Gradient Boosting (GradientBoostingRegressor) for recorded n1(+15) using the six local signals. It offers a course-level alternative to the graph/recurrent and adaptive-kNN predictors above. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [\hyperlink{ref:13}{13},\hyperlink{ref:14}{14}]; Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees but specifies no future lead [\hyperlink{ref:15}{15}], pp. 1-2. Our review did not identify this exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. The expected contribution is a reproducible comparison on the released SCADA, including operating/leak conditions. It would test a useful extension of the reviewed solutions; it is not a new algorithm or a claim of worldwide priority.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
{\footnotesize\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\hsize=0.57540\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\hsize=1.42460\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\rowcolor{Blue!13} \textbf{Planned output} & \textbf{What we expect to learn} \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}C1. Forecast-ready table} & Which past inputs belong to each +15 label; reproducible time-based splits. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}C2. Boosted-tree forecast} & Whether boosting reduces error relative to persistence, Ridge and Random Forest. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}C3. Auxiliary-signal assessment} & Whether tank, pump, peers and local demand add value beyond n1 history. \\
\end{tabularx}\par}
\vspace{1.7mm}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.2 Preparation, learning models and course scope}\par\vspace{.7mm}
Use 12 readings per signal: current, 5 min earlier, ..., 55 min earlier. These give 72 columns. Add sine/cosine of clock time and seven weekday indicators: 81 inputs, or 21 inputs for n1-only. These are derived columns, not extra sensors. Align timestamps/check units and missing values; set y to n1 three timestamps later. Remove incomplete rows and labels belonging to the next split. Fit Ridge scaling using training data only; leak labels stay outside X. Section 1 EDA motivates the baselines but does not prove that all six signals help.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\[\widehat p_{n1}(u+15)=F_M(X(u)),\qquad F_m(X)=F_{m-1}(X)+\eta h_m(X).\]
Boosting starts with the training-target mean and adds shallow trees that learn the remaining prediction errors; eta controls each correction [\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. The target remains absolute future pressure. This is the sequential error-correction idea of Module 11.6/Week 7. Ridge is a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients [\hyperlink{ref:16}{16}]. Random Forest averages trees fitted to resampled training rows [\hyperlink{ref:17}{17}]. Boosted trees can capture nonlinear relationships among pressure, tank storage, pump operation and local consumption; an advantage over these baselines must be tested.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
Initial model settings, to be checked on validation data: boosting stages \{100,200,300\} Ã— learning rates \{0.03,0.1\}, depth=2, minimum leaf=20, squared-error loss, subsample=1; no random-split early stopping. Forest: 200 trees, max\_features=1.0, depth \{6,10,14\} Ã— minimum leaf \{10,40\}. Ridge alpha: \{0.01,0.1,1,10,100,1000\}. Use tree seed 42 and six settings per learner/input set, chosen by validation MAE [\hyperlink{ref:16}{16},\hyperlink{ref:17}{17},\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. These are proposed choices, not proven best settings.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.3 Evaluation and criteria for improvement}\par\vspace{.7mm}
Train January-September 2018; choose settings October-December 2018; then refit on eligible 2018 and freeze settings before the 2019 assessment. Keep time order and the same forecast rows for every model, with 55 min of past readings. Remove labels that cross a split boundary; past inputs may come from the previous period. Since 2019 was already inspected, it is a final time-based assessment, not a blind test. Compare persistence, Ridge, Forest, full boosting and n1-only boosting. In validation, remove T1, PUMP\_1, n4/n31 or AMR\_n1 one group at a time, following the class attribute-removal idea.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
Recompute MAE/RMSE on these common 2019 rows; report errors in metres and skill=1-MAE\_model/MAE\_persistence (positive means better than persistence). A boosting advantage requires lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Forest; auxiliary value requires beating n1-only boosting and persistence. Limited or absent auxiliary-signal value is also informative: it supports a simpler model. Report monthly errors, RMSE tradeoffs and unsuccessful results; these comparisons do not prove statistical significance. Deliver the preparation/model notebooks, settings, score table and prediction/error plots. No learning model has been fitted. Delays/ages, balance proxies and block intervals remain outside this scope.\par\vspace{1.7mm}

Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction.\\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# PDF Skill\\r\\n\\r\\n## When To Use\\r\\n\\r\\n- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.\\r\\n- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.\\r\\n- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.\\r\\n- Validate final rendering before delivery.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Tools + Contract Requirements\\r\\n\\r\\nImmediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\nnode container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Workflow\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.\\r\\n   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.\\r\\n   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.\\r\\n2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.\\r\\n3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.\\r\\n4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Fill And Validate AcroForms\\r\\n\\r\\nVisual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.\\r\\n2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.\\r\\n3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:\\r\\n\\r\\n```python\\r\\nfrom pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter\\r\\nfrom pypdf.generic import NameObject\\r\\n\\r\\nreader = PdfReader(input_pdf)\\r\\nwriter = PdfWriter()\\r\\nwriter.clone_document_from_reader(reader)\\r\\n\\r\\n# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.\\r\\nwriter.reattach_fields()\\r\\nfields = writer.get_fields() or {}\\r\\nmissing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)\\r\\nif missing:\\r\\n    raise ValueError(f\\\"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}\\\")\\r\\n\\r\\nvalues_to_write = dict(expected_values)\\r\\nif flatten:\\r\\n    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.\\r\\n    values_to_write = {\\r\\n        name: field.get(\\\"/V\\\", \\\"/Off\\\" if field.get(\\\"/FT\\\") == \\\"/Btn\\\" else \\\"\\\")\\r\\n        for name, field in fields.items()\\r\\n    }\\r\\n    values_to_write.update(expected_values)\\r\\n\\r\\nwriter.update_page_form_field_values(\\r\\n    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten\\r\\n)\\r\\n\\r\\nif flatten:\\r\\n    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.\\r\\n    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes=\\\"/Widget\\\")\\r\\n    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject(\\\"/AcroForm\\\"), None)\\r\\n\\r\\nwith open(output_pdf, \\\"wb\\\") as stream:\\r\\n    writer.write(stream)\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.\\r\\n5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Temp And Output Conventions\\r\\n\\r\\n- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.\\r\\n- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.\\r\\n- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Dependencies\\r\\n\\r\\nPrefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. Your Anticipated Contributions}\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.5mm}\\\\labeltext{3.1 Anticipated contribution relative to prior work}\\\\par\\\\vspace{.7mm}\\r\\nWe propose Gradient Boosting (GradientBoostingRegressor) for recorded n1(+15) using the six local signals. It offers a course-level alternative to the graph/recurrent and adaptive-kNN predictors above. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [\\\\hyperlink{ref:13}{13},\\\\hyperlink{ref:14}{14}]; Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees but specifies no future lead [\\\\hyperlink{ref:15}{15}], pp. 1-2. It would test a useful extension of the reviewed solutions; it is not a new algorithm or a claim of worldwide priority.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n{\\\\footnotesize\\\\setlength{\\\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\\\\renewcommand{\\\\arraystretch}{1.16}\\r\\n\\\\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}\\r\\n\\\\begin{tabularx}{\\\\linewidth}{@{}>{\\\\hsize=0.57540\\\\hsize\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}X>{\\\\hsize=1.42460\\\\hsize\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}X@{}}\\r\\n\\\\rowcolor{Blue!13} \\\\textbf{Planned output} & \\\\textbf{What we expect to learn} \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}C1. These give 72 columns. Add sine/cosine of clock time and seven weekday indicators: 81 inputs, or 21 inputs for n1-only. These are derived columns, not extra sensors. Fit Ridge scaling using training data only; leak labels stay outside X. Section 1 EDA motivates the baselines but does not prove that all six signals help.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n\\\\[\\\\widehat p_{n1}(u+15)=F_M(X(u)),\\\\qquad F_m(X)=F_{m-1}(X)+\\\\eta h_m(X).\\\\]\\r\\nBoosting starts with the training-target mean and adds shallow trees that learn the remaining prediction errors; eta controls each correction [\\\\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. The target remains absolute future pressure. This is the sequential error-correction idea of Module 11.6/Week 7. Ridge is a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients [\\\\hyperlink{ref:16}{16}]. Random Forest averages trees fitted to resampled training rows [\\\\hyperlink{ref:17}{17}]. Boosted trees can capture nonlinear relationships among pressure, tank storage, pump operation and local consumption; an advantage over these baselines must be tested.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\nInitial model settings, to be checked on validation data: boosting stages \\\\{100,200,300\\\\} Ã— learning rates \\\\{0.03,0.1\\\\}, depth=2, minimum leaf=20, squared-error loss, subsample=1; no random-split early stopping. Since 2019 was already inspected, it is a final time-based assessment, not a blind test. Compare persistence, Ridge, Forest, full boosting and n1-only boosting. \textbf{Research gap.} Among the reviewed studies, none reports a directly comparable n1(+15) forecast evaluation on the released SCADA. Leak diagnosis, current reconstruction and forecasts with other data/horizons answer different questions. We therefore compare Gradient Boosting, persistence, Ridge and Random Forest on common chronological splits and test auxiliary-signal value. This provides reproducible classical baselines for future comparison with complex models, which are not reproduced here.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\newpage
\milestoneheader{2}{Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting}{4/5}
\sectiontitle{3. Your Anticipated Contributions}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.1 Proposed contribution}\par\vspace{.7mm}
We propose to forecast recorded n1 pressure 15 minutes ahead using Gradient Boosting and the six signals selected in Section 1: n1/n4/n31 pressures, T1 level, PUMP\_1 flow and AMR\_n1 demand. The contribution would be a reproducible comparison for this specific task using the released SCADA data.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
The reviewed graph and adaptive-kNN studies address related prediction tasks. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [\hyperlink{ref:13}{13},\hyperlink{ref:14}{14}], and Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees without specifying a future lead [\hyperlink{ref:15}{15}]. Our review did not identify this exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. We therefore propose an extension of the reviewed applications, without claiming a new algorithm or worldwide priority.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
{\footnotesize\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\hsize=0.57540\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\hsize=1.42460\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\rowcolor{Blue!13} \textbf{Planned contribution} & \textbf{Question to answer} \\
\textbf{C1. Prepared forecast data} & Can we organize past measurements and their +15-minute pressure labels without using future inputs? \\
\textbf{C2. Model comparison} & Does Gradient Boosting improve on persistence, Ridge and Random Forest? \\
\textbf{C3. Value of extra signals} & Do the tank, pump, peer pressures and local demand help beyond n1 history? \\
\end{tabularx}\par}
\vspace{1.7mm}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.2 Data preparation and models}\par\vspace{.7mm}
We will align timestamps, check units and missing values, and pair each input row with n1 three readings later. Starting inputs will include current readings and the previous 55 minutes at five-minute intervals, plus time of day and day of week. Only information available at forecast time will be used; leak labels will stay outside the model inputs. Rows without required values or with a target outside their assigned period will be excluded. Any scaling for Ridge will be fitted on training data only. Section 1 EDA motivates these choices but does not establish that every signal helps.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
Gradient Boosting adds small decision trees that try to correct earlier prediction errors [\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. We will compare it with persistence (use the current n1 pressure), Ridge (a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients) [\hyperlink{ref:16}{16}], and Random Forest (average predictions from many trees) [\hyperlink{ref:17}{17}]. These models build on regression and ensemble ideas studied in class. Model settings will be chosen using validation data; detailed parameter choices are left for implementation.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.3 Evaluation and expected improvement}\par\vspace{.7mm}
We will train on January-September 2018, use October-December 2018 to select settings, then refit on eligible 2018 rows and assess 2019. This preserves time order. Since 2019 has already been inspected, it is a final chronological assessment. All models will predict the same rows.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
We will report MAE (average absolute error) and RMSE (which gives more weight to large errors), both in metres. A boosting advantage would require lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Random Forest. To test the extra signals, we will also compare full boosting with boosting using n1 history and the same calendar variables. Extra-signal value requires lower MAE than this reduced model and persistence. We will report RMSE tradeoffs and cases where no improvement is found.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
The expected outputs are an annotated preparation/model notebook, a comparison table and forecast/error plots. These are planned experiments; no learning model has been fitted.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\newpage
\milestoneheader{2}{Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting}{5/5}

MILESTONE 2 ASSESSMENT

Description of Dataset and Learning Task: Very Good

Your description of the BattLeDIM dataset is thorough and technically informative. The supplied Zenodo link is valid, and the reported data volume, measurement types, and available variables are consistent with the dataset documentation.

Your proposed task is clearly defined as supervised regression: predicting pressure at n1 fifteen minutes ahead. The selected predictors and proposed construction of time-lagged features are appropriate. The dataset is sufficiently large for the proposed learning methods.

However, Section 1.3 explicitly presents substantial exploratory data analysis (EDA) as your own work, including numerical calculations, correlations, and figures. You also claim to have performed independent checks of the data tables and hydraulic network. No code or other reproducible record is provided to substantiate these claims.

Although performing EDA independently at this stage is permissible, it is not required for Milestone 2. If you choose to present it as original work, its provenance must be clear and verifiable.

Kaggle Contributions: Very Good

You provide a detailed and useful discussion of six relevant studies, including their learning tasks, data preparation, models, code references, evaluation measures, and reported results.

I appreciated your careful distinction between future-pressure forecasting, current-pressure reconstruction, and leak detection. These distinctions are important for determining which previous results are directly comparable with your proposed task.

Your claimed screening of 35 groups of studies is not accompanied by a complete inventory or a reproducible account of the screening process. Since Figure 4 is explicitly presented as your own analysis, you should provide supporting evidence or clarify the source of the information.

Your literature review should also consider Ahmed et al. (2025), "Windowed Multivariate LSTM Autoencoder for Leak Detection in Water Distribution Networks." This study uses the same five principal Area C measurements that you selected, although for a different learning task. Your proposed sixth signal and forecasting objective may still constitute meaningful extensions, but their relationship to existing work should be explained.

Your Anticipated Contributions: Problematic

The proposed supervised learning experiment is clearly specified, and the planned comparison of Gradient Boosting, Ridge, Random Forest, and persistence is technically feasible.

However, there is a serious unresolved issue concerning the originality of the analysis upon which your proposal is based. You repeatedly describe extensive computational results as your own, despite providing no code, notebooks, or other evidence showing how they were obtained.

This makes it difficult to determine which analyses you performed independently, which may have been reproduced or adapted from existing work, and which remain merely proposed. Consequently, the originality of the groundwork supporting your proposed contributions cannot presently be established.

This rating reflects the unresolved questions about attribution and provenance. It is not a determination that an Academic Integrity violation has occurred.

Your proposed contribution also needs a more convincing justification beyond applying established regression models to a narrowly specified forecasting task. Explain precisely what new understanding the experiments are intended to provide, and how the value of your proposed additional predictors will be demonstrated.

Presentation: Problematic

Your report is exceptionally well organized and visually polished. The explanations, tables, figures, and references are generally clear and concise.

However, professional technical reporting requires clear attribution and reproducibility, not merely polished presentation. Labeling figures and numerical results as "Own EDA" without identifying how they were produced is a significant reporting weakness.

The distinction between completed analysis, reproduced analysis, observations drawn from the literature, and proposed future work must be made explicit throughout the report.

Provide appropriate citations for externally obtained results and links to the code supporting any computational work that you claim to have performed.

ADVICE FOR THE NEXT STEPS IN YOUR PROJECT

First, please clarify the provenance of the EDA and other analyses presented as your own. If you performed these analyses independently, provide the corresponding code or notebooks, including the procedures used to generate Figures 2-4, calculate the reported correlations and persistence errors, examine the SCADA tables, and determine the hydraulic path distances.

If any of these results or figures were obtained from existing publications, repositories, tutorials, or other sources, identify those sources explicitly and revise the descriptions so that the authorship of each contribution is accurately represented.

Since Milestone 2 did not require implementation, you will not be penalized for work that has not yet been performed. It is important, however, not to represent anticipated work or externally obtained results as independently completed analysis.

For your final report, retain the useful discussion of previous models, but strengthen the explanation of how your contribution differs from existing studies. In particular, distinguish the novelty of the forecasting task, feature selection, experimental comparisons, and evaluation methodology.

Your reported persistence baseline already achieves a very small error. This suggests that improving on persistence may be difficult. Determine whether Gradient Boosting provides a meaningful improvement, and whether the additional signals improve prediction beyond what can be achieved using n1 history alone.

Ensure that all model comparisons use identical forecast targets, evaluation periods, and performance measures. Preserve chronological ordering, prevent future information from entering the predictors, and avoid selecting model settings using the final test period.

The proposed experiments are manageable for a four-week group project, provided that the group concentrates on reproducible data preparation, a small number of well-justified models, and carefully controlled comparisons.

Please proceed to Milestone 3 by advertising your proposal and forming a group. Before developing this proposal into the introductory material for your final report, however, the questions concerning the provenance and originality of the claimed analyses must be resolved.

- Michael Houle

Any scaling for Ridge will be fitted on training data only. We will compare it with persistence (use the current n1 pressure), Ridge (a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients) [\\\\hyperlink{ref:16}{16}], and Random Forest (average predictions from many trees) [\\\\hyperlink{ref:17}{17}]. This preserves time order. Since 2019 has already been inspected, it is a final chronological assessment. All models will predict the same rows.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\nWe will report MAE (average absolute error) and RMSE (which gives more weight to large errors), both in metres. A boosting advantage would require lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Random Forest. To test the extra signals, we will also compare full boosting with boosting using n1 history and the same calendar variables. Extra-signal value requires lower MAE than this reduced model and persistence. """Reproducible nominal-topology and training-period audit, without model fitting."""
from pathlib import Path
from collections import defaultdict
import json,hashlib,heapq
import pandas as pd
import yaml

W=Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1];M=W.parent/'MiniProject/Milestone 2'
D=M/'respaldo/datos/oficial';Z=D/'zenodo_4017659'
O=M/'respaldo/estado_del_arte/seleccion_features_v013';O.mkdir(parents=True,exist_ok=True)
sections={};line_refs={};sec=''
for num,line in enumerate((Z/'L-TOWN.inp').read_text(encoding='utf-8-sig').splitlines(),1):
    t=line.split(';',1)[0].strip()
    if t.startswith('['):sec=t.upper();sections[sec]=[]
    elif t and sec:
        row=t.split();sections[sec].append(row);line_refs[row[0]]=num
config=yaml.safe_load((Z/'dataset_configuration.yaml').read_text())
g=defaultdict(list);closed=[]
for r in sections['[PIPES]']:
    if len(r)>7 and r[7].upper()=='CLOSED':closed.append(r);continue
    g[r[1]].append((r[2],float(r[3]),r[0]));g[r[2]].append((r[1],float(r[3]),r[0]))
def component(root):
    seen=set();todo=[root]
    while todo:
        n=todo.pop()
        if n in seen:continue
        seen.add(n);todo += [b for b,_,_ in g[n] if b not in seen]
    return seen
junctions={r[0] for r in sections['[JUNCTIONS]']}
roots={'A':'n300','B':'n226','C':'T1'}
components={area:component(root) for area,root in roots.items()}
assert not (components['A']&components['B'] or components['A']&components['C'] or components['B']&components['C'])
upstream_junctions=junctions-set.union(*components.values())
assert upstream_junctions=={'n303','n336'}
dist={};queue=[(0.,'T1')]
while queue:
    d,n=heapq.heappop(queue)
    if n in dist:continue
    dist[n]=d
    for b,length,_ in g[n]:
        if b not in dist:heapq.heappush(queue,(d+length,b))
f=D/'csv_verificados/2018_SCADA_Pressures.csv'
p=pd.read_csv(f,sep=';',decimal=',',parse_dates=['Timestamp']).set_index('Timestamp').loc['2018-01-01':'2018-09-30']
assert len(p)==78624
areas={}
for area,nodes in components.items():
    sensors=[n for n in config['pressure_sensors'] if n in nodes]
    stats=[]
    for n in sensors:
        err=(p[n].shift(-3)-p[n]).dropna()
        stats.append({'node':n,'std_m':float(p[n].std()),'min_m':float(p[n].min()),'max_m':float(p[n].max()),'persistence_mae_15_m':float(err.abs().mean()),'nominal_distance_T1_m':dist.get(n)})
    areas[area]={'junctions':len(nodes&junctions),'pressure_sensors':sensors,'pressure_count':len(sensors),'AMR_count':len(set(config['amrs'])&nodes),'stats':stats,'median_sensor_std_m':float(pd.Series([r['std_m'] for r in stats]).median())}
valves=[{'id':r[0],'upstream':r[1],'downstream':r[2],'type':r[4],'nominal_setting_m':float(r[5]),'line':line_refs[r[0]]} for r in sections['[VALVES]']]
controls=[' '.join(r) for r in sections['[CONTROLS]']]
assert areas['C']['pressure_sensors']==['n1','n4','n31']
assert areas['C']['AMR_count']==82 and areas['B']['pressure_sensors']==['n215']
assert all(r['id'] not in c for r in valves for c in controls)
assert config['flow_sensors']==['PUMP_1','p227','p235']
hashes={str(path.relative_to(M)):hashlib.sha256(path.read_bytes()).hexdigest() for path in [Z/'L-TOWN.inp',Z/'dataset_configuration.yaml',Z/'README.txt',f]}
out={'period':'January-September 2018','rows':len(p),'future_test_2019_used':False,'models_trained':False,'topology_scope':'Nominal INP; connected components of initially open pipes, excluding pumps and PRVs. Components are hydraulic sectors in the nominal operating arrangement, not a new dynamic hydraulic simulation.','areas':areas,'valves':valves,'controls':controls,'closed_pipes':closed,'pump':[r for r in sections['[PUMPS]'] if r[0]=='PUMP_1'][0],'outlet':[r for r in sections['[PIPES]'] if r[0]=='p239'][0],'tank':sections['[TANKS]'][0],'flow_sensors':config['flow_sensors'],'screening_counts':{'all_measured_channels':119,'area_C_and_tank_inflow_candidates':87,'selected_base_channels':6,'excluded_upstream_pressure_flow_channels':32,'excluded_local_AMRs':81},'source_hashes':hashes,'source_record':'https://zenodo.org/records/4017659','interpretation_limits':['PRV settings are nominal model settings, not observed regulator-state/setpoint time series.','A fixed PRV downstream setting does not imply constant remote-node pressure.','The tank is a local head/storage boundary; full independence from upstream forcing is not established.','Nominal distance is not hydraulic sensitivity, signal delay or water travel time.','AMR_n1 is local nodal demand, not a pipe inflow meter or total sector demand.','Pump flow is tank inflow; prospective incremental forecast value remains a hypothesis.']}
out['upstream_boundary_junctions']=sorted(upstream_junctions)

name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.
